Generalised: **identify an act by what DEFINES it (a scrollbar drag is a press on the scrollbar), never by a temporal correlation with its effects — a time window admits every artifact that happens to land inside it and excludes every human slower than it.** Pins: scripts/test-chat-trim-guard.mjs (112, incl. a DOM-free unit over the extracted decision table, a FUNCTIONAL one that runs `setSuspended` hide→show against fake geometry (a transitional unpin still re-tails, a real reader is left alone), and round 4's units over the shipped `_pointerOnScrollbar` — content click false / gutter press true / no-scrollbar list false / RTL gutter on the left / border ≠ scrollbar / a uiScale-zoomed rect that the un-converted comparison would call a gutter press) + scripts/test-desktop-resume-paging.mjs (35) — a headless-chrome A/B on a real >34 MB transcript across a real desktop switch, whose NEGATIVE CONTROL rebuilds the bundle with the gates patched out and must show `extendTop:done` on the resume, through an input-less `scrollTop→0` sentinel probe, and must strand the window on the resume+1210 ms gap injection that the fixed build ends pinned at the tail; round 2 adds a nav-during-settle leg (a jump at +400 ms must STAY where the reader put it), a 1240/1400/1900/2400 ms input-less displacement sweep that must all end pinned at the tail, a real wheel-up at +1400 ms that must still page, and a PER-MECHANISM negative control for each — neutered on the instance in the same run, because the source-level control rebuilds with `RESUME_SETTLE_MS = 0` and cannot host a timing-dependent repro. Round 3 adds three TRUSTED-input legs at resume+300 ms on the same window — a real left-click at the list centre (the +1400 ms displacement behind it must still end pinned at the tail; control: give the click the old full `_endResumeSettle()` and it strands again), a real `mouseWheel` up (it must unpin IMMEDIATELY and page, and `unpinSkipResume` must never fire against a reader), and a real native-scrollbar drag (`mousePressed` on the 15 px scrollbar + `mouseMoved`, with `.chat-minimap-active` removed first because the semantic minimap hides the native scrollbar — it must trace `userPos/scrollbar-drag` and leave the reader where they dragged; control: neuter `_pointerDragScroll` and the same drag is pulled back to the tail, st 0 → 624). Round 4 adds two more on the same window, each with its own instance-level control: a click at +300 ms with the displacement at **+450 ms** (fixed: pinned at the tail; control: restore the pre-fix time-window signature on the instance — it strands the window), and a gutter press HELD 900 ms before the first move (fixed: `userPos/scrollbar-drag`, the reader stays at `fromBottom 1440`; control: make `_pointerOnScrollbar` answer false — the same drag is yanked back to `fromBottom 0`), plus trusted-input asserts that a content press is not a gutter press and that the flag is cleared on release. **ROUND 5 (B-9702, the residue the round-2 leg left behind — and the first round MEASURED before it was explained): (l) OUR OWN SCROLL CHAIN IS AN AUTOMATIC WRITER TOO.** Round 2 taught the repair to cancel its re-tail TIMERS the moment the reader positions the view, and left `_forceScrollToBottom`'s 10-frame chain running — the other automatic writer, and the very one round 2's own note blamed for the +1240/+1280 probes surviving by accident. Measured, not assumed: the round-2 leg "a REAL wheel-up at resume+1400 ms still pages" was run 20× on that build and the reader was dragged back to the live tail **5 times (25 %)**, with one trace shape every time — `userPos/wheel` at +1399 ms, then `repin st:1760 sh:2468 ch:708 structAge:1404 fsb:-1 posAgo:3` at +1402 ms, then `trimSkipPinned` → `pinnedRetail` → `extendTop:done`. Read: the +1240 ms rung's chain wrote `scrollTop = scrollHeight` **3 ms AFTER the reader's own scroll**, the pin re-engaged off OUR write, and the pinned-tail invariant (part 3 above) then faithfully re-asserted the bottom — the reader's page-up silently undone. The backlog's hypothesis, "the collapsed-geometry window re-pins", was REFUTED by the same capture: `sh - ch` was 2.5 viewports and `collapsedGeomSkip` never fired there. **FIX: (l) the chain carries an EPOCH and `_cancelForcedScroll(via)` bumps it** from `_notePositioning` / `_noteUserNav` (never from `_noteUserInput` — a click still moves nothing), so every frame already queued finds itself orphaned instead of writing once more; a later `_forceScrollToBottom` starts a NEW epoch, so a cancel can never kill auto-follow (its own unit control). **(m) A MUTE MUST OUTLIVE THE WRITE IT MUTES**: `_programmaticScroll` was cleared in the same frame as the chain's final write, and a scroll event is delivered AFTER the callback that wrote it — which is exactly why the capture reads `fsb:-1` at the re-pin, i.e. the guard whose whole job is to reject our own displacement had already stood down for it. The release now runs one frame later, guarded on `_fsbActive` so a chain restarted in that gap keeps its mute. Same 20-run measurement after the fix: **0/20**, `repin` never appears, and 9 of 20 legs show `fsbCancel … frames:9` — the chain caught on its last frame, which is precisely how narrow the race was. **(n) An assert that declines to name the USER-VISIBLE outcome is where a residue hides**: the round-2 leg checked only "something paged" and "the unpin gate didn't fire", explicitly reasoning that the final pin state "is not this gate's to decide" — so it passed on every run where the reader lost their page-up. It now asserts the reader's outcome on a settled snapshot (unpinned, away from the tail, `windowStart` moved up, no `repin`), plus a DETERMINISTIC form of the race — a chain armed by hand immediately before the wheel — whose per-mechanism control neuters `_cancelForcedScroll` on the instance and must drag the reader back to `fromBottom <= 8`. Generalised: **every automatic writer of scrollTop must lose to a positioning act (a timer was only one of them), and a guard against our own writes must outlive the last of those writes.**

## A MEMO KEY THAT NAMED NEITHER THE PRICES NOR THE LEDGER (2.369.43, review batch — the 2.369.36 interval memo's two holes)
The inc-mtox23xw fix memoized `costBetweenMulti` on `ids|from|to|events-at-or-before-to`. Both remaining holes were reproduced before fixing. ① PRICING: the key names the LEDGER but not the PRICE TABLE, and `_cost` reads `this._pricing` live — so after a pricing edit (a tier rate or a per-account discount, POST /api/usage-stats/pricing or a config import through persistence.js) every already-memoized historical interval kept returning pre-edit dollars forever, poisoning the estimator's learned rates and every anchor's `costSince` while the Usage window (which does not go through the memo) showed the new numbers. Repro: a 10× opus tier edit and a 50% account discount each left the total frozen at $3.50 against a truth of $35.00 / $17.50. FIX: `UsageHistory.pricingToken()` = a sha1 of {tiers, accounts}, recomputed only when `_pricing` is REPLACED (the same object-identity check `_tier` uses), appended to the key; a ledger object that cannot version its prices is NOT memoized at all. ② EVICTION: `learnRates` walks one identity's anchor pairs in the SAME order on every recompute — a cyclic reference string, the one access pattern where FIFO (and LRU) go off a CLIFF instead of degrading. Measured on the shipped code: a 20000-key working set hits 100%, 21000 hits **0.0%** — a 5% overshoot destroys 100% of the memo and the per-pair ledger walk of inc-mtox23xw comes straight back. Real anchor files are append-only forever (production: 5124 qualifying pairs over 28 days across 8 identities, ~180 new/day, biggest identity 1126), so the 20000 cap is reached in months, not never. FIX: a RANDOM victim (O(1) via a parallel key array) — the same input now holds 53% at 1.3× the cap. Also: the memo moved from a module global to a WeakMap per UsageHistory, so two ledgers can never read each other's costs through a key that names neither. **INVARIANTS: a memo over computed values keys on every INPUT of the computation, not just the data it walks (a rate table, a discount, a rounding rule are inputs); if an input cannot be versioned, do not memoize; an eviction policy is chosen against the ACCESS PATTERN — recency policies are worst-case for repeated full scans, and "cap exceeded" must degrade, not cliff; a cache keyed by values that name no instance belongs to the instance (WeakMap), never to the module.** Pins: scripts/test-usage-ledger-perf.mjs (21) — price-edit/discount/clear parity vs brute force, hit-path parity, cross-ledger isolation, and a hit-rate floor at 1.3× cap (both negative-controlled: dropping the token reddens 4, true FIFO reddens the hit-rate assert at exactly 0.0%).

## THE SHORT-VIEW RESCUE THAT COULD NEVER FIRE (2.369.43, review batch — dead since 2.369.36)
`loadHistory`'s post-attach auto-fill exists because a view whose content does not fill the viewport has NO scrollable range: the scroll handler never runs, and on touch there is no wheel-up path either, so a window sits on two run headers with thousands of unreachable messages above. The 2.369.36 hardening (a re-attach rebuild read sh<=ch on a TALL pinned window while content-visibility heights were unresolved, fired `_extendTop`, and a late slab + trimBottom cost the live tail) added `rendered < 30` to the gate. That predicate is UNSATISFIABLE: every attach path ships `tail(50)` (ws-handler's `_normalizer.tail(50)`, `transcripts.page`), `_windowStart > 0` holds EXACTLY when those 50 arrived, and the semantic fold HIDES members (`.chat-run-collapsed`, display:none) instead of removing them — so `rendered` is ~50 whenever the gate is armed. Measured over 33 real production transcripts with total>50: 50 of 50 tail messages render a `.chat-msg` child, min 50, none below 30. The rescue was dead code for everyone, and the fold-dominated case (inc-mtajy6wr's cousin: 50 cards collapsing to ~240px in a 700px viewport) had no way out on touch. RE-DERIVED from the intent instead of the symptom: the indeterminate geometry is TRANSIENT — the same premise `collapsedGeomSkip` runs on ("heights resolve within ~1.5s") — so CORROBORATE the reading: `_shortViewNeedsFill` is a named predicate over the injected list geometry, and `_scheduleAttachFill` measures at 700ms and again 900ms later, extending only if both readings agree AND `_lastStructuralAt` did not move in between (any paging/trim/desktop-resume hands the decision back to the machinery that owns it). The card count survives only as the HARM bound (`rendered + 50 <= 150`): the rescue adds ONE page and never at a size where a trim could eat the live tail. Since inc-mtq5bpjt-0o0n the whole schedule also DEFERS past the desktop-resume settle (bounded re-arms, and the second reading re-checks it) — a resume is the same class of artifact, and the resume path owns the tail while it is running. **INVARIANTS: a guard added under incident pressure must be shown to be SATISFIABLE on the path it guards — a condition that is always false is indistinguishable from deleting the feature, and nothing reports it; when the honest signal is transient noise, corroborate it across the settle window rather than proxying it with an unrelated quantity; DOM-free client predicates get functional tests (chat-view.js imports in plain node) — the pre-fix gate looked plausible in review and was arithmetically impossible in production.** Pins: scripts/test-chat-trim-guard.mjs (61) — the fold-dominated case fires, the tall/no-history/suspended/disposed/teleported/over-cap cases do not, and the two-reading corroboration is driven end to end (artifact ⇒ no extend, genuine ⇒ exactly one).
## CODEX .ZST / DISCOVERY / REMOTE-FETCH BATCH (2.369.x, review findings — four ways a codex thread went missing or came back wrong)

**① A RATIO GUESS DECIDED WHETHER A THREAD EXISTED.** `readHeadText` (discovery-facts) read a bounded COMPRESSED prefix and one-shot decompressed it under `maxOutputLength: max(plainBytes*4, 1MiB)`; anything compressing better than ~4× threw EZSTBIG, the `catch { return ''; }` turned that into an EMPTY head, `extractCodexThreadMeta` produced `threadId: ''`, and the thread VANISHED from /api/sessions — then the empty meta was cached by mtime, so for a stopped thread it stayed gone until a restart. Measured on the dev box: compressing the 40 real `~/.codex` rollouts at the DEFAULT zstd level, 7 reproduced it (ratios 4.0–6.0, e.g. plain 1,115,325 → comp 195,087). Fix: `zstdDecompressHead` BISECTS over compressed prefix sizes under a fixed output cap (a truncated prefix decompresses to the plain text it covers — verified on this runtime) until ≥ plainBytes of plain text exist; an unreadable head now THROWS a coded error (`EZSTUNSUPPORTED`/`EZSTHEAD`; zero plain bytes out of non-empty compressed input is a failure, not emptiness) so the existing `extractFailed` rule refuses to cache it, and `_noteExtractFailure` logs the reason verbatim once per file per 10 min. **Invariants: a bounded reader must be bounded by CONSTRUCTION (bisect/stream), never by a guessed compression ratio; "unreadable" and "empty" are different answers and only the second may be cached; a degrade path that hides a thread must say so in the log.**

**② A CACHE GUARD MEASURED THE WRONG CLOCK.** The codex rollout walk's per-directory listing cache (`codex-session-store._listDirCached`) skipped the readdir when the dir mtime was unchanged AND `now - mtime > 2s` — the coarse-timestamp guard evaluated at LOOKUP time. Settledness is a property of the CAPTURE: a listing taken while the mtime tick was still open can miss a sibling created in the same second, and that sibling never bumps the mtime again, so once the clock passed mtime+2s the stale listing was trusted forever (the poll is 5s — the window is always over by the next lookup) and the second rollout never appeared in the session list. Fix: record `settled` at fill time; an unsettled capture is re-read on the next lookup until it is taken settled (the readdir-per-poll win is untouched for settled captures). **Invariant: a freshness/settle predicate is evaluated and STORED when the data is captured — evaluating it at read time answers a question about the reader, not about the data.**

**③ "PLAIN WINS" WAS A COMMENT, NOT A RULE.** `interpretDiscoveryLines` de-duplicated a thread's `.jsonl` and `.jsonl.zst` twins by keeping the FIRST entry, commented "plain wins — first in mtime order". Both producers sort newest-first (hosts.js `sort -rn`, agentd.js `b.mtimeMs - a.mtimeMs`) and compression happens AFTER the last write, so the compressed twin was always first — and the ssh scanner can only read a compressed head where the host has `zstd(1)`, so the remote card silently lost the cwd/name its plain twin's HC/NC lines carried. Fix: group by thread id, prefer the plain twin's facts EXPLICITLY, MERGE what each twin has (either fills a gap), mtime = the newer of the pair, CO on either twin marks the one thread running. **Invariant: when two files describe one entity, the preference is expressed in the predicate, never inherited from an upstream sort; a dedup that drops facts must merge them instead.**

**④ ONE CACHE SLOT, MANY REMOTE FILES.** `hosts._fetchRemoteByFind` keys `data/remote-jsonl/<host>/codex/<tid>.jsonl` by conversation id, but codex's `remoteFind` predicate matches the plain rollout OR its `.zst` twin, so `find … | head -1` could resolve a DIFFERENT file across polls (the host compresses a finished rollout). The meta recorded only {size,mtime,fetchedAt,slab}, so the append-only delta path concatenated the new file's bytes onto the other file's cached prefix and stamped the result complete — and a stopped thread never changes again, so the corrupt transcript was served forever. Fix: the meta records `remotePath` + `compressed`; a changed path invalidates the slot (full refetch, no cache-valid short-circuit); a compressed remote — or an already-compressed cache file, detected by magic bytes so pre-fix metas are covered — is never delta-appended; the locate pipeline is `find … | sort | head -1` so the pick is deterministic and prefers the plain twin. **Invariants: an incremental/delta sync must verify the SOURCE identity, not just its size and mtime; a cache slot shared by several possible sources records which source filled it; a locate that may match several files sorts before it picks.** **④b THE FIX DID NOT HEAL WHAT WAS ALREADY BROKEN (same batch, review follow-up).** Provenance protects the slots the FIXED code writes — but every slot in the field already carried a meta without `remotePath`, and the first cut read that as “same file” (`!meta.remotePath || …` is vacuously TRUE), so a cache the old delta path had ALREADY spliced (plain prefix + the twin’s bytes, stamped complete) kept passing the size/mtime short-circuit — forever, since a stopped thread never changes again and nothing else invalidates the slot. A migration whose trigger is “the remote moved” never runs for the exact population it was written for. Fix: a meta WITHOUT provenance is NOT valid — refetch whole ONCE and rewrite it (and it may not delta either) — plus the cache FILE now testifies about itself: its head magic must agree with the resolved remote (zstd bytes under a plain remote, or the reverse, = the other twin’s bytes in the slot) and a plain cache whose last 4KB hold a NUL byte or a zstd frame header is spliced (an append always lands its foreign bytes at the TAIL). The ONE exception to “heal by whole refetch” is a remote already past `maxBytes` — a whole refetch is impossible there (the delta path is how such a slot grew), and failing “too large” on a transcript that worked yesterday is worse than trusting the byte check, so a verified cache adopts the provenance we just resolved. **Invariants: when a fix adds a field the correctness check depends on, records written BEFORE it are not “unknown, assume fine” — they are the corrupted population, and the missing field must read as INVALID; a self-heal must not be gated on the broken input changing; and a cache’s own bytes are stronger evidence than its metadata about those bytes.** Pins: scripts/test-codex-zst.mjs ①b/③/④b/⑥/⑥b (real-shaped >4× fixture at default level, twin order both ways, utimesSync same-tick repro, stub device switching remote files under one slot, and a pre-written hybrid cache + old-shape meta that heals with the remote standing still — each with the pre-fix predicate asserted as the negative control).
## ACP WRAPPER: STOP DIDN'T STOP, DENY RAN THE TOOL, AN EXIT-0 AGENT DIED SILENTLY (review batch over data/bin/acp-wrapper.js, after 2.369.31)
## ACP WRAPPER: STOP DIDN'T STOP, DENY RAN THE TOOL, AN EXIT-0 AGENT DIED SILENTLY — AND THE STOP THAT FIXED #2 SILENCED THE BOOKKEEPING NUDGE (review batch over data/bin/acp-wrapper.js, after 2.369.31)

Four defects in the generic ACP v1 client plus the round-2 follow-up ②b that the ② fix introduced, all reproduced against the REAL wrapper over pipes (scripts/dev/mock-acp-agent.mjs), all pinned in scripts/test-acp-harness.mjs (91).

**① Stop lost a race with the prompt-context fetch.** `runPrompt` claims the turn SYNCHRONOUSLY (so a second message queues) and only then awaits `fetchContextPrefix()` — a real HTTP round trip to /api/agent/prompt-context. A Stop landing inside that await saw `activePrompt`, flipped `cancelled` and sent `session/cancel`; the await then returned and dispatched the prompt anyway, so the wire read `initialize → session/new → session/cancel → session/prompt` and the agent — which had no turn to cancel — ran the whole turn the user had just stopped (and, like the mock, an agent may LATCH that cancel and kill the NEXT turn instead). Fix: `runPrompt` holds its own turn handle (`mine`) and re-checks `mine.cancelled` after the await, before dispatch: a cancelled prompt emits `prompt_end` with stopReason `cancelled` (shared `endPrompt` path, so streaming/meta/queue-drain stay identical) and is never sent. The interrupt verb also only emits `session/cancel` for a turn it actually dispatched (`mine.dispatched`) — an undeliverable cancel is worse than no cancel.

**② The interrupt verb kept the queue.** ACP has no queue verb, so the wrapper holds queued prompts locally; the old handler cleared them only in the `else if` branch that runs when NOTHING is active. Stop during a turn therefore cancelled it and the cancelled turn's own `drainPromptQueue()` immediately dispatched the queued prompt — the agent kept working, with a second permission request, right after Stop. Fix: interrupt ALWAYS splices the queue and says how many it dropped (`queue-cleared` notice). A queued PEER message was already reported delivered (`peer_result` ok/queued), so dropping it silently would lose a promised message: each dropped peer entry re-emits `peer_result {ok:false,text,fromName}` (the queue entry carries `opts.peerText/peerFrom` for exactly this) and the acp-events consumer re-stashes it on the delivery ladder.

**②b The dropped queue entry kept its latch (review round 2).** The stop-time bookkeeping nudge is FETCHED by `endPrompt` (a `/api/agent/stop-check` round trip) and dispatched only when the answer lands — by then `drainPromptQueue()` has usually started the next turn, so the nudge itself lands in the local queue. `nudgeTurnActive` (the guard that stops a second nudge from being queued behind the first) is cleared by `endPrompt` for the nudge's OWN turn, and a queue entry that never runs never reaches an endPrompt: the ② fix's unconditional queue drop therefore STRANDED the flag at true, and every later `end_turn` took the `!nudgeTurnActive` branch — one Stop at the wrong moment silently disabled the bookkeeping reminder for the rest of the session (no stop-check call, no notice, nothing in the journal to see). Fix: the interrupt loop resets `nudgeTurnActive` for every dropped nudge entry, and the `queue-cleared` notice counts only what the USER queued (telling someone to "send it again" for an internal nudge they never sent is a lie; the log line still carries the true total). **Invariant: per-turn state must die with the turn it belongs to — when a lifecycle hook (endPrompt) is the only place that clears a flag, EVERY path that can destroy that turn without running the hook has to clear it too.** Pinned by two asserts that go red on the old wrapper (`stopChecks` frozen at 1 after a later end_turn) plus a control that runs the same setup WITHOUT the Stop — green in both states, so it proves the harness sees nudges at all and that the fix is not just the latch deleted.

**③ A Deny selected an ALLOW option.** `resolvePermission`'s last-resort fallback was `msg.approved ? options[0] : options[options.length - 1]` — positional, not kind-checked. An ACP agent is free to offer only `[allow_once, allow_always]` (the option list is the agent's), and then the LAST option is `allow_always`: a rejection executed the tool AND stood the permission down for the rest of the session (reproduced: `{approved:false}` → selected `always` → tool_call_update completed). Fix: FAIL CLOSED — a rejection with no reject-kind option answers `{outcome:'cancelled'}` (ACP's own no-answer outcome; the tool does not run) and emits a `deny-cancelled` notice saying what the Deny became, because cancelled may end the turn rather than just refuse the tool; approvals keep the positional fallback. Today's desktop client only sends `approved:false` without an optionId from the option-less permission card, so this was a latent contract hole rather than a live UI path — the wrapper's stdin verbs are a documented contract and a Deny must never be answerable with an allow.

**④ An agent exiting 0 before `initialize` produced ZERO records.** The child-exit handler gated its boot-failure notice on `code !== 0`, and `finalizeExit()` calls `process.exit()` synchronously — so `boot()`'s own catch (the `boot-failed` notice) never runs from that path either. An agent that quits 0 before answering initialize (a CLI printing usage because the acp subcommand changed, an unauthenticated agent giving up quietly) left an empty journal and an empty buffer file: a blank chat window that never said why, the ACP twin of the "dead session = blank shell" class. Fix: the notice fires whenever the agent exits before the session is ready, whatever the code (text names `exit code 0` / `signal X`), and the journal IS the history so the buffer file carries it across a restart.

**INVARIANTS: ① a flag captured before an await is re-checked after EVERY await before the side effect it guards (a synchronously-claimed turn plus an async prefix is a race by construction) — and never send a control message for work the peer does not know about; ② "Stop" is total: local queues are part of the stopped state, and anything dropped that was already reported delivered goes back to the delivery ladder, never quietly discarded; ②b dropping a queued item also drops ITS per-turn state — a latch cleared only by the lifecycle hook of the turn that never ran outlives the session it was meant to guard, and a user-facing count never includes work the user did not queue; ③ a permission fallback fails CLOSED — a rejection may never resolve to an allow-kind option, and a rejection expressed differently than the user asked must SAY so; ④ exit code 0 is not success when the protocol handshake never completed — report the failure on the record channel the window rebuilds from, not only on a non-zero exit.**
## PLUGIN SECURITY BATCH — MANIFEST TEXT BECAME CODE, AND CONSENT COVERED THE WRONG THINGS (2.369.43, plugin-system review; all reproduced before the fix)
## PLUGIN SECURITY BATCH — MANIFEST TEXT BECAME CODE, AND CONSENT COVERED THE WRONG THINGS (2.369.43 + round 2 in 2.369.44, plugin-system review; all reproduced before the fix)
Seven findings in the Ph2/Ph4 plugin system, one theme: **a plugin manifest is UNTRUSTED INPUT, and consent is about a package, not an id.** ① *Generated shims interpolated free text into code*: `syncShims` wrote `// ${t.description.replace(/\n/g,' ')}` — only `\n` was stripped, so a lone **CR** (or U+2028/U+2029, which end a line for a JS parser) closed the `//` comment and the rest of the description became statements in `data/bin/vibespace-tool-<id>-<name>` — a file that runs OUTSIDE the plugin's `node --permission` sandbox, with `VIBESPACE_SESSION_TOKEN` in its env, and that SHIPS to every ssh host and dial device with the core agent tools (repro: `--help` alone wrote the attacker's file). Fix: `jsLiteral()` (JSON string + `U+2028`/`U+2029` escaped — the shims run under whatever node a remote host has) is the ONLY way manifest text enters generated code; description and args live in `const DESCRIPTION` / `const ARGS_SCHEMA`, the comment header carries only the regex-validated id/tool slugs; and `cleanText()` in the PURE validator strips control chars + line separators from EVERY free-text field (description, label, window title, tool description, setting/theme labels) at the one choke point. ② *Consent did not cover agent tools*: `server:true` + `contributes.agentTools` with no `capabilities` block was `needsConsent === false`, so those PATH-wide programs installed themselves silently — `needsConsent` now includes contributed tools and the summary item says they run outside the sandbox (the shipped hello-plugin example now needs consent too). ③ *Declared fs paths were prefix-tested unnormalized* while node's permission model COLLAPSES them: `//home`, `/home/u//vibespace/data`, `~//vibespace/data`, `/home/u/./vibespace/data` and `/./` all passed the forbidden-root check — and `--allow-fs-read=/.` grants the entire filesystem (verified against real `node --permission`). `normalizeFsPath` now expands `~`, collapses the path itself (PURE, no `require('path')` — the validator has ZERO requires by design) and refuses anything that still contains `//` or `/./`. ④ *Reinstall inherited trust*: `registry.enabled`/`registry.trust` are keyed by plugin id, so a DIFFERENT package declaring an already-trusted id came back auto-enabled and trusted (its client module served same-origin, its consented capabilities intact — a copied capability surface keeps `capabilitiesHash` identical, so the drift check never fires). The installer now fingerprints the staged package (`contentHash`, sha256 over `relpath\0size\0filehash`), and `install()` keeps the fast path ONLY for a byte-identical package from the same recorded source; anything else resets `enabled` + `trust` with a notice, and the install dialog says so. `update()` (the owner pulling a new version from the source they already chose) keeps consent and records the new fingerprint. ⑤ *Proxied route replies were documents on the app origin*: `/api/plugins/<id>/x/*` echoed the child's `contentType`/body with no `nosniff` and no CSP, so a consent-free plugin could serve HTML/JS **same-origin as VibeSpace**. Every proxied reply now carries `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` + `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox; default-src 'none'`, and a document content-type (html/xhtml/svg/xml) from an untrusted plugin is served as `text/plain` with one journal line. ⑥ *The intentional-stop mark was per RECORD*: `rec._stopping` was set by `stopChild` and cleared only when the OLD child exited (≤3s: SIGTERM→SIGKILL), so a freshly started child that crashed inside that window was filed as an intentional stop — no crash count, no `lastError`, no restart, panel says "stopped". The mark is now a Symbol on the CHILD. ⑦ *The uploaded `.vsp` leaked*: the `source` whitelist threw ABOVE the try/finally that removes the multipart temp file. Also fixed under the same review: `capabilitySummary` said "write files under X" while `permissionArgs` adds `--allow-fs-read` for every write path (now "write AND read"), and `stageFromPath`'s recursive audit + `cpSync` of ≤200 MB ran SYNC on the main thread against an owner-supplied path — now `fs/promises` with a bounded (depth-capped) walk, honoring the "never block the event loop" law. **INVARIANTS: ① free text from a manifest/registry/peer NEVER enters generated code except through a JSON literal with the JS line terminators escaped — and it is sanitized once, in the validator, not at each use site; ② a path grant is normalized the way the ENFORCER normalizes it before any prefix/containment test (the same class as any allowlist compared against an unnormalized string); ③ anything that installs a program on a session's PATH is a capability and needs consent, whatever tier declared it; ④ consent/trust is recorded for a package (content + source), never for a name — replacing the code behind a trusted id re-opens the dialog; ⑤ a proxied third-party response on the app origin is DATA: nosniff + sandbox CSP, never a live document unless the plugin is trusted; ⑥ a "we did this on purpose" flag belongs to the OBJECT being acted on, never to a record that can hold two of them.** Pins: scripts/test-plugin-security.mjs (48, one reproduction per finding: injected shim + real spawn, the exact path list, install-A/install-B trust reset, proxy headers, a hang-on-SIGTERM child racing a crashing successor, upload cleanup), plus the consent-gate updates in test-plugin-loader / test-plugin-trust.

**ROUND 2 (2.369.44) — the verifier's two survivors, both "the model was right, the MODEL of the enforcer was wrong":** ⑧ *The `*` wildcard was not modelled.* ③ taught `normalizeFsPath` to collapse a path the way node's permission model collapses it — but node also accepts a WILDCARD, and it truncates the pattern: it reads a declared path only up to the FIRST `*` and grants every path whose STRING starts with what came before it. Probed against real `node --permission` (v24): `--allow-fs-read=/t/a/*` AND `--allow-fs-read=/t/a/*/never-used` both read `/t/a/b/c/f.txt`; `--allow-fs-read=/t/a/z*` reads `/t/a/zz.txt` but not `/t/a/b/…` (a character prefix, not a path-segment one); `--allow-fs-read=/*` reads `/etc/hostname`. Because the forbidden-root test compared the pattern as if it were a plain path, `capabilities.server.fs.read: ["/*"]` and `["<parent-of-the-install-dir>/*"]` PASSED validation and handed a plugin process VibeSpace's own install and data dirs — the exact grant ③ existed to refuse, through a grammar nobody had modelled. Fix: the wildcard is split off before collapsing (`/dir/*` = a subtree, `/dir*` = a string prefix that also covers `/dirty` — collapsing the trailing slash away would have silently widened one into the other), at most ONE `*` and only as the last character (a mid-pattern `*` is refused LOUDLY instead of accepted as something narrower than it is), containment runs on the literal prefix (`r.startsWith(prefix)` — so a wildcard over a parent of a forbidden root, and a partial-segment `…/dat*` catching `…/data`, are both refused), `/` and `/*` are refused with or without roots, and an accepted pattern is stored VERBATIM so the string the owner consented to is the string node gets. ⑨ *The reinstall trust reset was gated on the FOLDER.* ④'s rule is "consent survives only for a byte-identical package from the same recorded source", but the implementation read `r.replaced && !(sameSource && sameContent) && …` — and `r.replaced` only says a directory was overwritten. `enabled` and `trust` live in `data/plugin-registry.json` keyed by id and OUTLIVE the directory: with a hand-deleted plugin folder (or a prior install that failed after the registry write), there was nothing to replace, `replaced` was false, and a DIFFERENT package installed under that id came up pre-enabled and pre-trusted — its `client: module` served SAME-ORIGIN, no dialog anywhere. Fix: the gate is the registry record alone (`!(sameSource && sameContent) && (enabled[id] || trust[id])`); the notice is worded for both shapes ("a different package under the same id"). **INVARIANTS (added to the six above): ⑦ an allowlist entry is validated against the ENFORCER'S GRAMMAR, not just its normalization — if the enforcer accepts wildcards/globs/prefixes, the containment test models them, and a pattern whose effective grant is wider than it reads is refused loudly rather than accepted; ⑧ a security decision keyed on persistent STATE is gated on that state, never on a side effect that usually accompanies it (`replaced` is a fact about a directory; consent is a fact about the registry — the record outlives the folder).** Pins: scripts/test-plugin-security.mjs (62 — the wildcard refusal list + a legitimate wildcard as the negative control + the real installer refusing a parent-of-install-dir wildcard; hand-delete the folder, reinstall a different package → disabled/untrusted/module 404, then the same package from the same source → consent kept). Both were verified RED against the pre-fix code before the fix landed.
## OPENCODE SERVE INDEXED THE WHOLE MACHINE (2.369.50, owner's production instance on 2.369.42)

Two hours after the S9 serve keeper lazily started `opencode serve` (cwd = the server process's own cwd, `/home/<user>`), that serve sat at **157-169 % CPU (209 CPU-minutes), VmRSS 5.0 GB, 33 threads, /proc `rchar` +14.3 GB in 4 s** (3.6 GB/s page-cache reads). Hottest thread `fff-watcher-own` (442 412 ticks) beside `notify-rs inotify`, **30 021 inotify watches**, all on one device — `/tmp`. The db was 244 KB and `/global/health` answered in 20 ms: it was not hung, it was **burning**.

**What actually caused it (measured with /proc against a real 1.18.29 serve, not inferred):** OpenCode boots an "instance" per DIRECTORY, and each instance recursively indexes + inotify-watches that whole tree. Which of OUR calls boots one is not obvious, so it was measured route by route:
* `GET /session`, `GET /session/:id`, `GET /session/:id/message`, `GET /project`, `GET /session?scope=project&directory=/` — boot **NOTHING** (0 fff threads, 0 watches, 60 s idle after each).
* **every** `GET /api/session/{id}/…` route (message, history, context, the session itself) boots an instance for that session's `location.directory`: +19 `fff-*`/`notify-rs` threads, +5 process threads, +180 MB RSS, and a recursive watch of the tree.

2.369.42's NAMING lookup (`firstUserMessage`) used the v2 `/api/session/:id/message?limit=3&order=asc` because it returns messages oldest-first. The owner's store held exactly one session whose `directory` was `/tmp` — a shared dev box's `/tmp`. The session had no user message, so the 60 s name-retry poked it forever. **The incident report's own hypothesis (our `directory=/` project query keeping the instance hot) was REFUTED by measurement — that query costs nothing; the naming route was the whole story.** The `cwd=$HOME` start is a real second weakness (it makes the serve's default project the `'/'` catch-all) but it indexed nothing on its own.

**Fix, four layers.** ① **Naming moved to the v1 list** (`GET /session/:id/message`, the WHOLE conversation oldest-first — v1 `limit` is the NEWEST N so it cannot page from the front), byte-capped at 1 MiB via a new streaming `maxBytes` in the client (six unbounded whole-conversation fetches into the server process is an OOM waiting to happen; a conversation past the cap has long since earned a real OpenCode title to fall back on). ② **The serve runs from its own empty directory**, `data/opencode-serve/cwd`, made into a throwaway git repo — measured: OpenCode's project resolution walks UP for a git worktree, so a plain directory inside the VibeSpace checkout resolves the WHOLE checkout (node_modules and all) and a *fake* `.git` directory does not stop the walk; with no repo above it the project is the `'/'` catch-all. ③ **`listAllSessions` takes the directory-less listing first** (verified: a bare `GET /session` is store-wide regardless of the serve's cwd; the v2 `GET /api/session` is the same set with cursor pagination and also needs no directory) and only issues `scope=project&directory=` for worktrees that pass `bootstrappableWorktree` — never `'/'`, `$HOME`, the tmp dir, nor the vcs-less `'global'` catch-all row (a real project row carries `vcs`, which is the API's own "is a real project" signal — no fs call, so the never-block-the-event-loop law holds). `forkSession`, the one call that hands a directory to a mutating endpoint, now calls `POST /instance/dispose?directory=` afterwards (verified: the forked session stays readable). ④ **A resource guard in the keeper**: the child's own `/proc` is sampled every 60 s; >150 % CPU sustained ≥5 min, or RSS >2 GB on any single sample, STOPS the serve, parks the locator `parked:runaway`, logs the numbers + what to turn off, fires telemetry `opencode-serve-runaway`, pushes `harness-store-updated` (BACKEND_META.storeReason + a toast) and refuses to respawn for an hour. Plus **boot self-heal**: a recorded serve whose `GET /project/current` worktree is `'/'` or `$HOME` (a 2.369.42 leftover, and its record has no `cwd`) is SIGTERMed and replaced from the isolated cwd — the owner's instance heals on update with no manual step. New setting `agents.opencodeServeAutostart` (default ON = the shipped behaviour) is read through a function, so `VIBESPACE_OPENCODE_SERVE=0/1` still wins, `VIBESPACE_SKIP_AGENT_HOOKS=1` still forces off, and a live flip needs no restart. **SUPERSEDED 2026-09-07 (owner decision, the real closure of this incident): a background third-party daemon must be OPT-IN, so the setting and the smoke belt are both DELETED and the switch is the built-in `opencode-serve` PLUGIN, DEFAULT OFF** — `decideAutostart({env, pluginWantsUp})` in the shared module is the one decision (the `VIBESPACE_OPENCODE_SERVE` ops override still wins), an explicit disable STOPS the process (including one merely adopted from the record), and the first time a user touches OpenCode the product offers the service once (per instance, broadcast) with the sidebar admitting that stopped conversations are hidden until they say yes. Invariant added: **the default answer to "may we run a daemon on the user's machine?" is NO — a resource guard makes a runaway survivable, it does not make an unrequested daemon acceptable.** **Two follow-ups from the adversarial review of that change (2026-09-07), both invariants in their own right:** ① **flipping a default flips what every derived condition MEANS.** `/api/home` reported the store as FAILED on `parked || (!ready && autostart === false)`; the second half was the ops kill switch alone while autostart defaulted ON, and became the NORMAL state the moment the service turned opt-in — so the client's "a park must never be silent" red toast fired on every single page load (fresh instance, after "Not now", and on instances that never had OpenCode). The predicate is now ONE function shared with the live push (`storeFailureReason`, src/server/cli-env.js) and fires only on a PARK; deliberately-off travels as `row.service` and is shown passively. Corollary: when you flip a default, re-read every condition that mentions the old one — and when two code paths answer the same question (a boot fetch and a live push), make them literally the same function, because the loud one will be the one that drifts. ② **a kill switch must cover ADOPTION, not just starting.** The reuse rung runs before the autostart gate, so `VIBESPACE_OPENCODE_SERVE=0` stopped us spawning a serve while happily adopting the one that outlived the last restart — and the panel, correctly locked because the env forces it off, then offered ZERO controls for a running third-party daemon. "Off" means the process is gone: under the switch a recorded serve is SIGTERMed + forgotten (the existing replace path), and `install()` runs that ladder once at boot so an instance nobody polls does not inherit one either.

**Same-source A/B on the owner's real store** (one serve, one session at `directory=/tmp`, the only variable being which route the naming lookup uses), 60 s each:

| | new loop (v1) | 2.369.42 route (v2), SAME serve |
|---|---|---|
| CPU | 1.9 % | 10.2 % and climbing |
| VmRSS | 458→334 MB | 334→**515 MB** |
| threads | 13 | **32** |
| `fff`/`notify-rs` indexer threads | **0** | **19** |
| inotify watches | **0** | **27 336** |

**INVARIANTS.** ① A third-party daemon we start is a RESOURCE we own: sample its `/proc`, bound it, stop it, and SAY SO — "healthy" answers to `/health` say nothing about what it is doing to the machine, and a background process that burns for two hours in silence is the same class of defect as a silent failed user action. ② Never hand a long-lived agent daemon the server's own cwd or `$HOME`: what it resolves from that cwd is what it will index. ③ When a daemon's cost depends on WHICH route you call, measure the routes — the API shape (v1 vs v2, "the small one must be cheaper") is not evidence, `/proc` is; the plausible hypothesis in the incident report was wrong and only measurement caught it. ④ A directory-less listing beats a per-directory one whenever both exist; a directory you pass to a foreign daemon must be a REAL project (its own metadata says so), never `'/'`, `$HOME` or a tmp dir. ⑤ A whole-response read into the server process gets a byte cap. Pins: scripts/test-opencode-serve.mjs (131; instance-boot modelled in the mock, spawn-cwd spy, self-heal, runaway with a fake /proc reader, dispose, the byte cap) + the manual measurement mode `node scripts/dev/opencode-serve-live.mjs --measure [seconds]`.

**Two bystanders found while getting the gate green** (both pre-existing on master, both position/path-coupled fixtures): test-quota-source pinned `src/backend-caps.js` as the LAST entry of test-architecture's PURE set, which went red the moment 2.369.43 appended `search-card.js` — now a membership match; and test-writer-sweep held the transcript fd in ITS OWN process while the sweep script's guard is `ps -o args=` matching `*claude*`, so under an agent worktree (`…/.claude/worktrees/…`) or any checkout below a directory named `…claude…` **the suite SIGTERMed itself** (status null, no stderr, stops mid-run) — the holder is now a child with a fixed, claude-free command line in `os.tmpdir()`. **Invariant: a fixture must not depend on where the repo lives, and a test that can kill itself will, on someone's machine.**

## CODEX EXCLUDED FROM THE FRAME-FILE BYPASS BY BACKEND ID (design-harness-plugins §1 P1, after 2.369.17)
The 2.360.0 frame-file bypass (big chat frames ride data/chat-frames/, stdin carries a `_frame_file` pointer — the fix for the 79928a2b 38MB pty-shredding poisoning) carried `session.backend !== 'codex'` in its condition, because codex-chat-wrapper had no `_frame_file` verb. So for codex the pre-2.360.0 world never ended: a multi-image paste (the client sends the same claude-shaped `{type:'user'}` frame with base64 image blocks to every backend; CodexAdapter.formatChatInput wraps it into one `chat-input` line) rode raw pty stdin at any size, and when the channel shredded it the wrapper's stdin loop hit `if (!msg) continue;` — the message was lost and NOBODY was told (no log line, no event, no toast); the capability gate's honest ">1MB refused, old wrapper" path never ran either, because the backend-id branch short-circuited before it. FIX (three touches, one contract): ① codex-chat-wrapper resolves `_frame_file` on its stdin path — read, unlink ALWAYS (never an orphan), must parse as ONE object, nested pointers refused, then dispatched through `handleInput` exactly as a stdin line (so the user record + turn/start are the normal chat-input path); it advertises `caps: { peerMessage, frameFile }` in the sidecar; every failure (unreadable/shredded/nested frame, unparseable raw line) logs AND emits a `task_failed` event, which CodexMessageManager renders as a system error card telling the user to resend; it also emits `_stdin_ack` on stdout the moment a stdin line arrives (the design doc's P2 "stdin ack" row, pulled in here because the bypass depends on it: ws-handler's broken-pty detector re-sends the line after 5s without an ack or buffer growth, and a re-sent pointer finds its file already consumed — a spurious error on top of the pre-existing duplicate-turn symptom; the server's codex-events stdout branch already consumed the ack, only the wrapper never sent it). ② ws-handler's bypass condition is size + local transport only (`>64KB && !session.host && session.socketPath`); the wrapper's advertised capability decides pointer / raw ≤1MB / evidenced refusal — the SAME three-way outcome for every backend, so an old codex wrapper now gets the honest "Terminate + Resume" refusal instead of a silent loss. ③ test-chat-frame-guard §2b drives the REAL codex wrapper against a stub app-server (pointer → intact 200KB image in turn/start input; shredded/missing/nested files and an unparseable raw line → four loud task_failed events, wrapper alive, next normal line still works) and §4 pins the gate shape (negative pin on the backend-id branch). INVARIANTS: ① a transport-safety feature (bypass, guard, refusal) is gated on the RUNNING wrapper's advertised capability, never on a backend id — a backend-id exclusion is a permanent "this backend is still broken" branch that also hides the gate's honest refusal; the registry law (backend-caps.js, peerDelivery) applies to wrapper stdin verbs too; ② a wrapper's stdin loop never drops a line silently — an unparseable line is a lost user message and must reach the user through the same error channel as any other stdin-handler failure; ③ a new stdin verb is added to BOTH wrappers (or declared unsupported in caps) in the same change — the twin-file class ("twin-sets = 0 is a metric") includes data/bin/chat-wrapper.js ⇄ codex-chat-wrapper.js.

## CODEX SANDBOX CLOSED LOOPBACK → AGENT TOOLS DEAD (2.369.17, design-harness-plugins §1 P0)
codex's `workspaceWrite`/`readOnly` sandbox policies default to `networkAccess:false` and the seccomp filter enforces it on 127.0.0.1 (measured: EPERM, `CODEX_SANDBOX_NETWORK_DISABLED=1`; AF_UNIX blocked too — a unix-socket agent API would NOT have helped). Every vibespace-* tool POSTs to VIBESPACE_API on loopback ⇒ in every non-yolo codex chat the agent was taught tools that could not run and nudged each Stop for bookkeeping it could not do. Fix: wrapper policies carry `networkAccess:true` when VIBESPACE_API is set; terminal path `-c sandbox_workspace_write.network_access=true` (BARE boolean — `pushCodexConfigOverride` JSON-quotes and would make it a TOML string); boot probe = `codex sandbox -- true` (functional, async, assume-supported until answered) instead of a PATH lookup that "failed" on npm installs and ran terminal 'default' unsandboxed while chat was sandboxed. **Invariants: a capability the product depends on (network to our own API) is verified by running it, not by looking for a file; the same permission mode must resolve to the same sandbox on every spawn path (chat vs terminal); `-c` overrides are TOML-typed.** Forensic tell found while pinning it: a child spawned by Node inside the network-disabled sandbox prints NOTHING (exit 0) — Node's stdio "pipes" are AF_UNIX socketpairs and the seccomp filter swallows writes to them, while the same command under a real shell pipe prints ERR-EPERM; any probe of sandboxed processes must report through a regular file. Pins: scripts/test-codex-sandbox-net.mjs.

## ATTACH STORM STALL → HEARTBEAT KILLED THE CLIENT → KILLS LOST (2.369.16, userW inc-mtndq0vb "无法打开也没法 terminate，卡死了")
The session was never stuck: dtach/wrapper/claude alive, socket connectable. The server had self-updated 3 min earlier; after a restart every chat session's FIRST attach ran a SYNC `convertHistory` over its whole transcript on the main thread (58 sessions, 1.35GB, 10–56MB each) and a desktop switch re-attached 19 windows → ~4 min of event-loop stalls (attach-acks in 32s bursts, `attached` 4 min late). The 30s heartbeat then judged a pong it could not have read during its own stall, terminated the live client, and the frames queued unread on that socket (second attach, both kills) died with it; the client reconnected into another round. Fix: ① `convertHistoryAsync` (both normalizers) + `normalizers.rebuildHistory` (single-flight, `_rebuildQueue` holds live records and replays them in order, flag after success) + `feedLive` as THE live-feed gate; ② `src/server/ws-heartbeat.js` — a tick >5s late terminates nobody (re-ping, judge next tick); ③ kill acknowledged to the requester (`killed {ok}`) and re-sent by the client across reconnects (`app.killSession` = `ws.request` resend:true; zero bare kill sends remain). Review-hardened the same release: rebuilds are FIFO (round-robin slicing lands every window at the total time), the rebuild window re-acks every 10s with progress and the client ladder extends on fresh acks; attach re-checks liveness after the await; peer cards + auto-resume writes go through `feedPeerCard`/`feedLive`; failed rebuilds drain the held queue; view-only/subagent/HTTP converts are async and `transcript-service.view` joins an in-flight rebuild; the heartbeat has a 1s loop-gap pulse detector (tick lateness alone missed a 30–35s block right after a ping) and a 6-round bound; the kill ack carries the REQUESTED id (`resolvedId` = remapped) and the client disarms its watchdog and sends the backendSessionId fallback as a one-shot (a resent fallback frame would kill the resumed successor). **Invariants: no synchronous whole-transcript work on the main thread anywhere (ws, HTTP, view-only); a liveness verdict must exclude our own stall from the measurement window — measured by the loop, not by the tick; every writer into a session normalizer goes through feedLive/feedPeerCard; a user command written to a socket is re-sent until acknowledged, the ack names the id the client sent, and the resent form must be idempotent against successors; after any await in a handler, re-check the session is still the one you started with.** Forensic tell: attach-ack (synchronous) arriving tens of seconds after send = loop stall, not network; `attached` for other sessions still arriving = socket alive. Pins: scripts/test-attach-rebuild.mjs (26).

## STRANDED WRITES UNDER A DISCONNECTED MOUNT POINT (2.369.15, owner's OneDrive "is not empty, use --allow-non-empty")
Not a stale fuse endpoint — the mount point held REAL files: the task store's generated `.vibespace/TASK.md` recreated `<mp>/…/AIContext/.vibespace/` on the bare local directory while OneDrive was disconnected (the constructor's boot regeneration runs BEFORE mounts exist, and every task change regenerates). Those files "vanish" the moment the storage reconnects (shadowed under the mount) and rclone refuses the reconnect; `--allow-non-empty` would only have hidden them. Fix, three layers: ① connect (`_ensureMountpointDir(mp,{quarantine:true})`, rclone AND cephfs paths — a kernel mount shadows silently) moves the whole directory aside to a sibling `<mp>.stranded-<ts>/`, recreates the mount point empty, broadcasts a level-2 server-notice naming path + items — never delete, never merge; the mount-point EDIT path never quarantines (a user may point a record at a populated directory on purpose). ② `mounts.shadowedBy(p)` = the registered storage whose mount point contains p but is NOT mounted (gmail folders + credential-only records excluded); files.js refuses every op under it with 503 "“X” is not connected — this folder is its mount point". ③ task-groups `_syncTaskMd` consults the `isPathShadowed` dep (lazy TDZ-safe getter, same shape as getPortForwards) and skips; `syncAllContextMd()` re-runs once after mounts exist and on every `mounts-updated` broadcast (the wiring tees it), so the skipped file lands the moment the storage is back. **Invariants: any server-side writer into a user path asks `shadowedBy` first; a non-empty mount point is quarantined, never deleted; a dead fuse endpoint (ls fails/times out) is NOT quarantine material — it falls through to the stale-daemon kill.** Pins: scripts/test-mount-stranded.mjs (26).

## THE FOURTH READER SITE — EVERY STOPPED OPENCODE CONVERSATION OPENED EMPTY (2026-09-07, B-eac2)
"View History" on a stopped OpenCode conversation showed **"No messages in this session's transcript yet."** while `opencode serve` had the whole conversation. S9's store-backed reader (`OpencodeServeSessionMessages`) has no bytes until `prepare()` has run — the essay even says so — and `transcript-service` awaits it at its **three** reader sites. The ws `attach {viewOnly:true}` branch is a **fourth** site, and it called `sm.raw()` without preparing. The failure mode is exactly the one the design chose on purpose ("a caller that does not await prepare gets an EMPTY reader, never a wrong one"), which is why it was silent: no error, no log, no telemetry — just a conversation that looked empty. It shipped with S9 and survived a 135-assert suite because every node-level test called `readConversation`/`transcript-service` directly; nothing opened the window a user opens.
**Fix**: `if (typeof sm.prepare === 'function') await sm.prepare();` before `sm.raw()` in that branch, with the failure logged verbatim.
**Invariants**: ① a lifecycle hook that "every reader site awaits" needs the SITES enumerated in the gate, not the count asserted in prose — grep every `createSessionMessages(` call site when adding one; ② "empty, never wrong" is a safe DEGRADATION and therefore an invisible BUG — a degrade path that cannot be distinguished from a legitimate empty result must be tested through the surface the user touches; ③ the thing that found it was a **browser leg driving the real feature end to end** (open the conversation, right-click a message, confirm the dialog, read the toast) — the node suites were all green.

## OUR OWN CLICK REPORTED AS SOMEONE ELSE'S TURN — and two more from the S9-remainder round 3 (2026-09-07, B-eac2)

**① THE "external" LABEL ACCUSED THE USER OF BEING A STRANGER.** Piece (f) marks a conversation `external` when its `time.updated` moves "while we were not the ones moving it" — and "we" was implemented as *a live session of ours holds this id*. Every mutation we make through our OWN serve routes on a **stopped** conversation (roll back, restore, answer/reject an ask) moves that same field, so clicking "Roll back to before this message" made the row claim another process was driving it for 90s. Reproduced 3/3 through the real product routes against a real 1.18.29 serve (`stopped` → `external` within 6s; the one run that missed had never listed the row before, and production lists it every 5s). User-visible, all confirmed against the rendered UI: the card dims to opacity .7 and loses its click-to-focus cursor, its title reads "Running in unsupported terminal (PID ?)", a yellow EXTERNAL badge appears, `session-card.js`'s `when:` HIDES the Fork… row, and a sidebar whose `defaultStatusFilter` excludes 'external' loses the conversation entirely. The branch's own suite had PINNED the bug as correct behaviour — it asserted `status === 'external'` immediately after its own `facts.revertTo(...)`, labelled "a real change we did not drive".
**Fix**: `noteOwnWrite(id, session)` + `ownWriteVerdict` in the facts. Recognition is **by VALUE** wherever the action returns one — MEASURED: `revert`/`unrevert` hand back the row's FINAL `time.updated` and 1.18.29 never bumps it again (0ms delta at +250ms…+8s) — so the listing that reports exactly what we wrote is ours however late it lands, an older value (a listing already in flight while we wrote) keeps the entry, and the first value PAST ours is somebody else's and says so on that very tick. Only the actions that return no Session (an answered ask) fall back to a 10s clock window, and they now re-read `/question` even for positional answers because the question is the only thing that names the conversation they are about to move. Fork writes nothing down, and that too is measured (forking does not move the SOURCE row).
**Invariants**: ① "someone else changed it" must be defined as *not us*, and **us includes every mutation our own product routes make** — a live-session check only covers the conversations that have a process here, which is precisely the set this feature exists to serve; ② prefer recognising your own write **by its value** over a clock window: the response already carries the new stamp, and a window is a blind spot in exactly the direction ("a real TUI wrote 2s after my click") the feature must not lose; ③ measure whether the server keeps moving the row after answering you before choosing the window at all; ④ a green assertion that says the buggy behaviour is correct is worse than no test — when an assert's label ("a real change we did not drive") contradicts the two lines above it, the label is the bug report.

**② KICK() DID NOT WAKE THE SLEEP IT WAS SUPPOSED TO INTERRUPT.** The live lane's reconnect loop backs off up to 30s; `kick()` (fired on the locator's ready edge — "the plugin was just enabled", "the serve respawned on a new port") reset `attempts` and aborted the fetch controller. During a backoff there is no fetch to abort, and nothing cleared the `setTimeout` the loop was sitting in, so the already-scheduled wait ran to completion and only the NEXT one was short. The service ships OFF, so this is the FIRST-USE path: measured 25.1s from "serve ready" to "lane healthy" end to end (11.0s to the next connect attempt in the unit shape), and while the lane is unhealthy the 10s list timer this whole piece replaced is the only source of freshness.
**Fix**: the sleep keeps its resolver; `kick()` and `stop()` clear the timer and resolve it before aborting. 51ms after the fix.
**Invariant**: an "interrupt the wait" primitive must end EVERY state the waiter can be in — a socket AND a timer. A regression that drives a fake lane (`kick(){kicks++}`) proves the call happened, never that it worked; the leg must reach a real ≥3s backoff so a sub-second reconnect cannot be an accident of a short timer.

**③ A PTY THE SERVE NO LONGER HAS IS AN EXIT, NOT A DROPPED SOCKET.** The serve terminal stayed open ~12s after the shell exited, logging five bogus warnings: the bridge treats every socket close as transient and burned all five reconnect rungs (800+1600+2400+3200+4000 ms) against an upgrade that answers a permanent HTTP 404. Measured on the wire: after `exit`, `GET /pty/<id>` is `PtyNotFoundError` and the ws upgrade is a plain 404, which the `ws` client reports as `error: Unexpected server response: 404` then `close`.
**Fix**: PURE `ptyGone(message)` (404/410 terminal; 502/503, ECONNREFUSED, ECONNRESET, "socket hang up" transient) sets a flag in the error handler that short-circuits `close` into one honest log line + `onExit`. The mock serve now answers 404 for an upgrade to a pty it no longer has, so the end-to-end leg asserts ONE attempt and an exit under a second.
**Invariant**: a bounded-retry policy needs a TERMINAL class — retrying a verdict only postpones the truth and buries the log; and the fixture must model the real refusal (the mock used to accept every upgrade, which is why the retry storm was invisible).


## A SERVE TERMINAL INDEXED THE USER'S REPO — and two more from the S9-remainder round 4 (2026-09-07, B-eac2)

**① "OPEN A TERMINAL IN THIS SESSION" RE-INTRODUCED THE 2.369.42 RUNAWAY, ON THE ONE NEW PATH THAT TAKES A DIRECTORY FROM THE USER.** `openPty` passed `directory=<the conversation's cwd>` to `POST /pty`, and `?directory=` is precisely what makes the serve boot an OpenCode *instance* for that tree — a recursive index plus an inotify watch of every directory in it. `closePty` only sends `DELETE /pty/{id}`; nothing in the pty family releases an instance (`forkSession`, 110 lines earlier, calls `client.disposeInstance(dir)` for exactly this reason). Same-origin A/B on a real 1.18.29 serve — fresh serve per arm, isolated HOME, target = a 200-directory / 4046-file git repo, /proc `Threads` + `grep -c '^inotify wd' /proc/<pid>/fdinfo/*` + `VmRSS`:

| arm | base [thr, wd] | after create | after `DELETE /pty/{id}` |
|---|---|---|---|
| WITH `?directory=` (what shipped) | [16, 0] | [44, **204**] | [41, **204**] |
| without it (body `cwd` only) | [16, 0] | [45, **4**] | [42, **4**] |

Three open/close cycles on the same directory never dropped below 204; only `POST /instance/dispose?directory=…` released it (threads 45→40, watches gone). And `readlink /proc/<shell-pid>/cwd` was the target directory in **both** arms — the query buys the SHELL nothing, the `cwd` **body** field is what places it; the query only decides which instance owns the registry entry. Watches ≈ directory count, so one terminal on a `node_modules`-bearing repo reproduces the 30 021-watch shape of the original incident, and the only backstop is the runaway guard, whose remedy is to SIGTERM the whole serve and park it for an hour (every stopped OpenCode conversation becomes unlistable).
**Fix**: the whole pty family (`ptyList`/`ptyCreate`/`ptyGet`/`ptyResize`/`ptyRemove`/`ptyTicket`/`ptyConnectUrl`) sends no `directory`; they all land on the DEFAULT instance, which is our own empty throwaway repo (the 4 watches above). **They had to change TOGETHER**: the pty registry is per instance — measured, `PUT /pty/{id}?directory=X` on a pty created without the query answers 404 `PtyNotFoundError`, and vice versa — so a half-migrated family is a terminal that opens and can then never be resized, closed or reaped. The mock serve now models both facts (an instance is booted by the QUERY only, never by the body `cwd`; the registry — including the ws upgrade — is per instance) and the real-binary leg measures the /proc watch delta with the old shape as its control.
**Invariants**: ① every path that hands a USER directory to a long-lived third-party daemon is the 2.369.50 class until measured — API shape tells you nothing, `/proc` does; ② a resource an API lets you CREATE but not release through the same family is not "cleaned up by close", and the right move is usually to never create it rather than to add a dispose; ③ when a resource id is scoped by a query, the whole family sharing that id is one atomic change — verify the cross case (the other query 404s) so a half-migration cannot be green.

**② A SERVE-OWNED PTY OUTLIVES US, AND NOTHING REAPED IT.** `session._opencodePtyId` was written in `ws-create` and read **nowhere**; `OpencodeServeClient.ptyList()` was never called. A serve pty is deliberately not dtach-restorable (`socketPath` is null, so boot-restore never sees it), and the locator's `process.once('exit')` only SIGTERMs a serve *we* spawned (`if (ch) ch.kill(...)`). So on a SIGKILL/OOM restart — or on **any** restart while the serve was ADOPTED from `data/opencode-serve.json` (`state.child === null`) — the serve survives and every pty it owns survives with it, with nothing left in the product able to reach or kill it. Independently confirmed on the real serve: a pty stays `running` and re-connectable after its websocket is closed, and only 404s once its own shell exits.
**Fix**: `facts.reapPtys()` — the adopt-or-reap ladder the dtach/job paths already use — on the READY EDGE of each serve **process** (`${pid}:${port}:${startedAt}`, idempotent: a routine notify must not re-sweep and race a terminal being opened). It asks the directory-less `GET /pty` and keeps `live.ptys` (every pty THIS process opened, registered *before* the ticket call so the open→session-register window is never a kill window) ∪ `heldPtyIds()` (server.js reads `session._opencodePtyId` → cli-env → the facts: the field's first consumer). A failed listing does not mark the serve swept, so it retries on the next ready edge.
**Invariants**: ① a registry field with no reader is a fix that was never wired — `grep` the field, not the intent; ② anything we create on a daemon that outlives us needs an adopt-or-reap decision at the moment the daemon becomes reachable, which is the only instant "who still holds this?" is answerable; ③ the keep set must include what THIS process just made, not only what the persisted model records, or the sweep races its own product.

**③ THE LIVE LANE COULD LIE ABOUT ITSELF, TWO WAYS.** `laneHealthy()` switches the list-refresh fallback OFF when the SSE lane is connected AND the store watch is `active` — and `active` was true for any directory that merely *existed*, while `armWatch()` ran at `start()` (before any serve could exist) and **latched** (`watchTried` was reset only when the watch attached nowhere), so the documented "the dirs are resolved LAZILY, once the serve answers `GET /path`" never ran on any machine where `$HOME/.local/share/opencode` already exists — i.e. every machine that has ever run opencode. Reproduced with the real product wiring against a real 1.18.29 serve: `state().liveLane.watch` named the SERVER process's store, `liveLaneHealthy` was true, and a session created by a SECOND opencode process on the serve's REAL store never appeared (8s of 100 ms-spaced `discover()` calls, **zero** `GET /session` issued because the lane claimed health); the same probe pointed at the serve's actual store saw it in 493 ms. In the default deployment both resolve to the same directory (`agentEnv()` keeps HOME and XDG_DATA_HOME), so this was a latent gap plus a false health signal rather than a shipped outage — but the false `active` is the worse half, because it is the flag that turns the fallback off.
**Fix**: `createStoreWatch` refuses a directory with no `opencode.db` (recorded in `failed`; `active` stays false ⇒ "unknown" ⇒ the timer fallback stays on, and the next reconnect re-arms because the serve creates the db at boot). `armWatch(why)` RE-RESOLVES on every `connected` against the serve's own `GET /path` home and re-arms only when the dir set actually DIFFERS (`watchedDirs`, plus a `rearms` counter in `state()`), so a reconnect storm cannot churn the watchers.
**Invariants**: ① a health flag that other machinery *disables a fallback* on must be positive evidence of the thing it names — "the directory exists" is not "this is the store"; ② "resolved lazily, once X answers" is a comment, not a mechanism, whenever the eager path can succeed: check that the lazy branch is REACHABLE on a normal machine; ③ blind is worse than slow — when a signal cannot be validated, degrade to unknown and keep the dumb timer.

## A DEAD ASK STAYED ANSWERABLE, AND AN OFF SERVICE STILL WATCHED THE STORE — S9-remainder round 5 (2026-09-07, B-eac2)

**① THE WARM QUESTION MAP OUTLIVED THE SERVE THAT MINTED IT.** `readConversation` joins an OPEN `question` tool part in the transcript to the live `que_…` request id, so a pending ask survives a page reload as an answerable card; it re-reads the authoritative `GET /question` only when the warm map is **empty**. But `/question` is answered out of ONE serve process's own memory — measured on the real 1.18.29 binary: a fresh serve answers `[]` regardless of what the previous one held — and the lane's reconnect handler refreshed the *busy* map (`statusMap()`) and not the questions. So after a serve restart (a keeper respawn on a new port, an OOM, an adopted instance replaced) the map was warm and entirely dead, every open ask was joined to a `que_…` nobody holds, and the card rendered with a Submit that could only 404: the exact dead Submit the `stale` marker exists to prevent. Reproduced both ways — against two mock serves with the product's own adoption path (card stayed `requestId: que_live1`, `stale: false`), and against the real binary (SIGTERM the child, keeper respawns, `state().pendingQuestions` still 1 while the new process's `/question` is `[]`).
**Fix**: the map carries the serve PROCESS it was filled under (`live.questionsFor = ${pid}:${port}:${startedAt}`, the same key the pty reaper uses). A map stamped for a different process is not "known", it is **unknown**: `pendingQuestionsFor` answers `[]`, so `readConversation` re-reads the authoritative list and marks the card `stale`; `answerQuestion`/`rejectQuestion` take their cold-map re-read path; `state().pendingQuestions` stops claiming asks that belong to a process that is gone. And every lane `connected` now DROPS the map before re-reading it (`refreshQuestions`) — drop first, so a failed read leaves "unknown" (⇒ stale, honest) rather than "known" from a serve that no longer exists.
**Invariants**: ① a cache of a REMOTE process's in-memory state must be keyed by that process's identity, not by the endpoint's URL — a respawn on a new port is a different fact source wearing the same name; ② whatever a reconnect re-reads because "we may have missed events", it must re-read **all** of it: a handler that refreshes one derived map and not its sibling is a silent half-recovery; ③ when a re-read fails, the honest state is UNKNOWN — a stale "known" is what turns a disabled button into a broken one.

**② WITH THE BACKGROUND SERVICE OFF, THE LIVE LANE STILL WATCHED THE USER'S OPENCODE STORE.** `install()` started the lane unconditionally, and `lane.start()` arms an `fs.watch` on the store dirs resolved from the env THIS server runs under (`armWatch('boot')`) plus an SSE reconnect loop with its own timers. Since 2.369.59 the OpenCode background service ships OFF and the rule is that nothing of ours runs or watches until the user turns it on — yet a fresh instance whose owner never enabled OpenCode held an inotify watch on `~/.local/share/opencode`, so an unrelated `opencode` process's writes woke our server for a feature that was off. Measured on a REAL boot (fresh `data/`, plugin at its default, isolated `HOME`): the unfixed server holds **1** inotify wd on the store dir (`/proc/<pid>/fdinfo`), while its `sse.lastError` reads "the OpenCode background service is off".
**Fix**: the lane follows ONE predicate — the same decision the keeper spawns on (`opts.autostart` = `decideAutostart`: the ops override, else the plugin record) **or** a serve that is actually READY, which covers the one case that decision does not: a RECORDED instance we merely ADOPT (allowed with the plugin off; once we are talking to it, following it costs nothing new). Every locator state change re-evaluates it, so enabling the plugin brings the lane up without a restart and disabling tears it down — SSE socket, backoff timer and store watch. Pinned by a real boot with the plugin off (0 watch handles, no record, not even the throwaway `data/opencode-serve/cwd`), whose POSITIVE CONTROL is the same instance with the plugin turned on through the real route (watch appears, then is released again on disable).
**Invariants**: ① "default OFF" means no timers, no sockets and no fs handles — a feature that only *refuses to act* while still subscribing is not off; ② the thing that decides whether a background client runs is the same predicate that decides whether its server runs, read through a function so a plugin toggle needs no restart; ③ a "we hold nothing" claim needs a positive control in the SAME process and the SAME measurement, or zero is indistinguishable from a broken probe.

## A SERVICE THAT WAS TURNED OFF KEPT THE WATCH IT WAS STILL ARMING — S9-remainder round 6 (2026-09-07, B-eac2)

Round 5 made the live lane follow the service: `install()` arms it only while the OpenCode background service is on, and a disable calls `lane.stop()`. That closed "the lane was never allowed to start". It did **not** close "the lane was already starting". `lane.start()` fires `armWatch()` and the SSE loop WITHOUT awaiting them, and both are `async` before they own anything they could release:

* `armWatch()` awaits `resolveDirs()` — which asks the locator for the serve's own `GET /path` home on a 2s budget, and that budget is spent **in full** exactly on the machine where the serve is parked or cannot boot, i.e. the state a user who is turning the service OFF is most likely in. `stop()` could only drop what was already attached, so the continuation then ran `watch = createStoreWatch(...)` on a lane nobody holds: an `fs.watch` on the user's real OpenCode store that no `stop()`, and no `uninstall()`, can ever reach again.
* `connectOnce()` awaits `locator.client({budgetMs: 8000})` before it creates its `AbortController`, so a `stop()` landing there aborted a controller that was still `null` (or the *previous* connection's) and the continuation opened a brand-new `/global/event` subscription. Its ~10s heartbeats keep it fed, so it never even trips the 45s idle abort.

**Measured, unfixed, three ways.** ① Through the real product wiring (`install()` → `locator.start()` → `locator.stop()`) with a serve that never answers: disabling at 0 ms and at 1200 ms both left **1** inotify wd on the store (`/proc/self/fdinfo`, matched by the store dir's inode) — and the leaked watch was **live**: a later write to `opencode.db-wal` fired `onExternal`, which in the product is a `markDirty` → an `opencode-updated` broadcast → every open client re-running the `/api/sessions` sweep, for a feature the user had turned off. ② Same result with the keeper genuinely PARKED after its five crashes. ③ On a real mock serve, a stop inside `locator.client()` left the serve holding an **open** SSE response, while `lane.state().sse` reported `stopped: true` **and** `connected: true` at the same time — a lane contradicting itself about whether it exists.

**Fix — `stop()` is TERMINAL, not merely "no new work"**. The lane gets `laneStopped`, set FIRST in `stop()`, and every async arm re-checks it at the point where it would take ownership: `armWatch()` returns early on entry (a queued `connected` frame must not re-arm a dead lane) and, after the await, hands the built watch to the ONE attach point — `attachWatch()` closes it and returns null instead of storing it. `resolveDirs()` skips its second round trip (`c.paths()`) when the lane died during the first: an in-flight call cannot be cancelled, but a stopped lane owes the serve no further requests. `connectOnce()` checks `state.stopped` after `locator.client()` (before creating the controller) and again after the fetch settles (an abort no longer cancels a body that has already arrived). The lane is single-use by construction — `stopLive()` drops it and `armLive()` builds a fresh one per enable — so "stopped" is safely a one-way door, and `kick()` is a no-op on it.

**Invariants**: ① a stop is terminal for work already IN FLIGHT, not just for work not yet started — every `await` between "we decided to acquire" and "we own it" is a window in which the owner can disappear, so the check belongs at the ACQUISITION point, not only at the entry; ② give a resource exactly one attach point, because that is the only place that can refuse to attach it to something dead; ③ an object whose lifecycle is "created per enable, dropped per disable" should say so and make stopped irreversible, rather than leaving a half-dead instance that can still act; ④ "we released it" is provable only with a NEGATIVE control that leaks — this branch's suite runs every leg twice, once against `src/opencode-events.js` and once against a copy of that same file with exactly these guards removed (asserted to match once each, so a control that stops neutering anything FAILS instead of quietly turning the A/B into two green arms).


## A SERVICE THAT WAS TURNED OFF STILL ADOPTED A SERVE, AND BUILT A FRESH WATCH FROM IT — S9-remainder round 7 (2026-09-07, B-eac2)

Round 6 made the live lane's `stop()` terminal for an arm already in flight. It fixed the lane. It did not fix **who starts the lane**: nothing the user clicks does — `install()` starts it from what the KEEPER publishes (`laneWanted = st.ready || wantsAutostart()`), and the keeper's `locate()` tests `state.stopping` **only at ENTRY**. Every rung below that entry reaches `adopt()` — the one place `state.client` is ever assigned — after an `await` that `stop()` cannot cancel:

* the reuse `/global/health` probe against a RECORDED serve that outlived a VibeSpace restart (1.18.29 answers in ~1.2s cold; a busy one takes hundreds of ms), then `unsafeReuseReason`'s `GET /project/current` on top of it;
* `ensureServeCwd`'s `git init` for the isolated serve cwd;
* the ≤20s boot wait after a spawn — which is exactly the "starting…" window a user gives up in and clicks Disable.

**Measured, unfixed, through the real wiring** (`serve.install()` → `locator.start()` → `locator.stop({killRecorded:true})` — the `_ocStart`/`_ocStop` pair in src/plugins.js — with a real `createLiveLane` over a real store dir): a Disable landing inside the reuse probe left the locator reporting `ready: true` **and** `stopped: true` at once, `source: 'reused'`, the runaway-guard interval `stop()` had just cleared RE-ARMED, and a BRAND-NEW live lane built — whose own `laneStopped` is `false` by construction, so round 6's guard could not help. That lane attached **1** inotify wd on the user's real `~/.local/share/opencode` and it FIRED: a later write to `opencode.db-wal` produced an `onExternal` ⇒ `markDirty` ⇒ `opencode-updated` broadcast ⇒ a fleet-wide `/api/sessions` sweep, for a feature the user had just turned off — while the published client pointed at a process `stop({killRecorded})` had already SIGTERMed. The same shape reproduced with the disable landing inside the boot probe (`source: 'spawned'`, same leak). Two neighbours in the same family: a stop landing inside `git init` still walked on and SPAWNED a brand-new third-party daemon after "off" (it was refused in production only by luck — `_ocStop` writes the plugin record before calling `stop()`, so the re-read `autostartOn()` happened to be false; a stop that is authoritative must not depend on a SECOND fact), and the boot loop's `return null` walked away from a child it had just spawned, leaving a live `opencode serve` plus a record the NEXT boot would ADOPT.
